# Shina: pending overnight review

**Approved and saved:** all numbered proposals in this review were saved on 10 September 2026. The tables and inventory below preserve the overnight review snapshot. Unresolved follow-ups have no accepted assignments. See [save validation](../approved-save.json).

All proposals remain approved and saved. Numbering is local to the full-Shina remainder pass.

## Straightforward (134)

| # | Shina | Proposed etymology | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **ek, ik** ‘one; One; 1’ | **\*ēkka**; [CDIAL[2462]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=119) | Kohistani, Guresi, Astor, Palas, and Chilas ek/ik retain k, matching Turner’s Shina ek under subsection 2 *ekka. The unstrengthened eka branch has contrasting loss of k in several northwestern languages. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 2 | **dū** ‘two; Two; 2’ | **dva**; [CDIAL[6648]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=380) | Palas, Gurez, and Chilas dū two agree with Turner’s Kohistani, Guresi, and Palasi dū under the dva paradigm. There is no additional formative requiring a derived numeral. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 4 | **chār, ʦ̣ār** ‘Four; 4’ | **catvā́ri**; [CDIAL[4655]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=252) | Chilas chār four agrees with Turner’s Shina čār under catvāri, subsection 2. Its long a distinguishes it from the caura/cōr outcomes assigned to caturaḥ, subsection 3. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. The HKAT affricate symbols are retained as source notation, not silently replaced by the other sources’ ch spelling. |
| 5 | **puś, poś** ‘Five; 5’ | **páñca**; [CDIAL[7655]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=431) | Chilas puś five matches Shina poš and Guresi nasalized poṃš under pañca. Retention of the sibilant distinguishes this dialect form from the more reduced Gilgit poi. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. The poś forms match Turner’s explicit Palesi poš and Gurezi pō̃š, rather than requiring the Gilgit nasal-vowel form. |
| 6 | **śah, ṣa** ‘Six; 6’ | **ṣáṣ**; [CDIAL[12803]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=743) | Chilas śah six agrees with the Guresi and Palasi ṣah forms under ṣaṭ, subsection 1. There is no labial element selecting the distinct *ṣuvaṭ branch. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 7 | **satt, sat** ‘Seven; 7’ | **saptá**; [CDIAL[13139]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=760) | Chilas satt seven matches Turner’s Kohistani/Guresi satt under sapta. The reduced pt cluster is independently represented in Middle Indo-Aryan satta. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 9 | **ná̃õ, nau, nãv, naũ** ‘nine; Nine; 9’ | **náva**; [CDIAL[6984]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=401) | Kohistani ná̃õ and Chilas nau nine match the Shina nau/nāũ numeral family under nava². They remain separate from the nava¹ adjective new. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 10 | **daī, dāe, day** ‘Ten; 10’ | **dáśa**; [CDIAL[6227]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=356) | Chilas daī ten closely matches Turner’s Shina dai under daśa. This is the inherited reduced numeral, not a compound built from smaller modern numbers. Additional HKAT entries glossed with Arabic numerals are grouped here with their equivalent written-out numeral attestations; original forms, numeric glosses and dialect tags are preserved. |
| 11 | **śall** ‘Hundred’ | **śatá**; [CDIAL[12278]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=711) | Chilas śall hundred agrees with Turner’s Shina šal under śata. The final l is therefore supported by the primary comparative evidence rather than silently corrected to another dialect’s śo. |
| 12 | **aʦ̣ʰī, aśī** ‘eye; Eye’ | **ákṣi**; [CDIAL[43]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=3) | Palas and Gurez aʦ̣ʰī match Turner’s Shina ac̣hī eye under akṣi; Chilas aśī agrees with the Palasi aṣī outcome in that article. The affricate and sibilant variants are documented within the family. |
| 13 | **kōṇ, kun** ‘ear; Ear’ | **kárṇa**; [CDIAL[2830]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=144) | Palas kōṇ and Chilas kun ear match Turner’s Shina kon/kuṇ under karṇa. The nasal stem supports this comparison; the anomalous Gurez kuṭu ear record is excluded. |
| 14 | **hat, hattʰ** ‘hand; Hand’ | **hásta**; [CDIAL[14024]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=811) | Palas and Gurez hat and Chilas hattʰ hand agree with Turner’s Shina hat/hath variants under hasta. Loss of s and differing aspiration are already represented among the dialect comparanda. |
| 15 | **don, doni, dōnī** ‘tooth; Tooth’ | **dánta**; [CDIAL[6152]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=352) | Palas don and Gurez/Chilas doni tooth match Shina don with plural dōnye under danta. Source-defined final-vowel variants remain separate attestations of the same dental stem. |
| 16 | **śīś** ‘Head’ | **śīrṣá**; [CDIAL[12497]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=724) | Chilas śīś head agrees with Shina ṣīṣ/ṣiṣ under śīrṣa. The final sibilant identifies the ṣ-bearing head formation rather than an undifferentiated link to śiras. |
| 17 | **bī, bī̌** ‘20; twenty; Twenty’ | **viṁśatí**; [CDIAL[11616]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=677) | Palas and Gurez bī twenty match the reduced Shina bi/bīh forms in Turner’s viṃśati article. These are simple score numerals; their higher compounds require component analyses. Three additional bī attestations, with source-specific tone or stress notation, agree with Turner’s explicit Shina bī series. Their original dialect and source metadata are preserved. |
| 18 | **lēl** ‘blood’ | **\*lōhila**; [CDIAL[11168]](https://dsal.uchicago.edu/cgi-bin/app/soas_query.py?page=650) | Palas and Gurez lēl blood exactly match Shina lēl under *lohila. Turner explicitly prefers this formation over lohita for the Shina liquid-bearing pattern. |

The final vowel is retained as a dialectal form rather than treated as a separate derivation. |

## Coverage

509 approved proposals cover 1297 records with 1752 proposed assignment rows. 6416 records remain unresolved or unexamined in the full Shina remainder.

Pending rows validate jointly with the earlier Shina proposals. No accepted overlay or compiled data changed. This pass includes records from all Shina dialects, including open Gilgit and Dras material.

Anomalous Gurez kuṭu ear is held for source review; it was not assigned to karṇa on the basis of its English gloss.

## Contact cases held for research

9 cases cover 118 records. These cases have no proposed assignments. Difficulty describes the remaining review; some moderate cases have an explicit source analysis but await an eligible donor or base node.

| Difficulty | Shina | Records | Evidence and next check |
|---|---|---|---|
| Difficult | **śā̌ko, śāko** ‘arm’ | 26 | Degener s.v. šáako gives Arm followed by [Bur. -śák], without an explicit direction arrow. Berger p. 388 has a damaged/merged OCR entry containing -ák and Arm; p. 389 preserves guśák in an arm-breaking curse. These establish a comparative lead, not a sufficiently verified immediate-donor assignment. CDIAL 12376 śākhā branch and 12379 śākhya do not themselves list this Shina arm term. |
| Difficult | **jū̌̃, jū̌, jua, źū̃, ʣ̣ũve, źuve, jũ, jũẽ** ‘louse’ | 10 | CDIAL 10512 lists Shina ǰū with the explicit qualification lw. with ǰ-. The selected jū/źū/źuve forms belong to that comparative problem, but the entry does not establish the immediate donor. Retain the source coronal and inflectional distinctions and investigate the donor before proposing an ancestry edge. Rajapurohit Dras jũ is added to this donor search: CDIAL explicitly labels Shina jū a loan with j-. Its compiled gloss says plural while its dialect tag says singular, so number also needs source checking. The further Rajapurohit jũẽ plural louse response is included in the same loan investigation. |
| Difficult | **gān, gāṇ** ‘leg’ | 7 | Degener s.v. gáan identifies the lower leg and compares Burushaski -ġáan heel, but does not mark borrowing direction. The body-part semantic relation is plausible; it is insufficient to choose an immediate donor without checking the Burushaski entry and contact history. |
| Difficult | **ā̂̃ṭe, ā̂ṭe, āite, ā́ṭe** ‘flour’ | 5 | CDIAL 1338 explicitly lists Shina ā̃ṭe flour as borrowed from an Indic source (← Ind.). The exact immediate donor is unspecified, so these survey forms are not given reflex links to *ārta. The anomalous āite notation also requires a source check. Rajapurohit āṭe is merged across flour and dough prompts; both meanings are retained, while the immediate donor remains unresolved. |
| Difficult | **cʰīmo, cʰūmo, cʰūmū, cimo, cʰimo, cūmo, cīmo, cūbo, śībo, cʰibo, cʰibū, śibo, cibū, chímɨ, chúmo** ‘fish’ | 31 | Schmidt and Kaul (2008), Example 5, resolve borrowing direction for Gilgiti, Kohistani and Drasi; eight records have moved to pending proposal 293. The remaining dialect records still need their own contact and transcription assessment. Degener’s comparison alone does not resolve those routes. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: pp. 244–246, Example 5. |
| Difficult | **úruk, úrke, urúk** ‘wolf’ | 3 | CDIAL 12062’s uncertain Jijal vk comparison with vṛka is revised in numbered addendum 14808 to a probable Burushaski loan. That note separately gives Domaaki urk from Sanglechi wvrk. These are distinct proposed routes: it does not establish the donor of the modern Dras and HKAT uruk/urke forms, which must not be linked directly to vṛka on resemblance alone. |
| Difficult | **puśī, puśō, buśī** ‘cat’ | 3 | Degener compares Shina būši cat with Burushaski bus, without a direction arrow. The LSI puśī/puśō/buśī series may involve an expressive animal-name family or contact, but the comparison alone does not demonstrate an immediate Burushaski donor or explain the initial voicing alternation. |
| Difficult | **á̃ṭi, áṭṭi, áṭi, ãṭʰi̥, ãṭi, aṭu̥** ‘bone’ | 6 | CDIAL 958 aṣṭhi explicitly places the Shina ā̃ṭi/aṭi bone series under its statement that the remaining Kafir and Dardic forms are borrowed from Indic, citing Morgenstierne. The source does not identify a precise immediate donor, and the remote relation of aṣṭhi to asthi is itself uncertain. These modern nasalized, aspirated and plain variants must therefore remain donor-unresolved rather than be assigned an unqualified ancient reflex. |
| Difficult | **śodī, śodo, śadī, śādo, śadī́, śadyḗ** ‘monkey’ | 27 | Berger’s printed-p. 388 entry compares Shina śōdo and feminine śōdi with the Burushaski monkey word; its OCR head is damaged to adí. Yoshioka supplies the intact Burushaski śadí comparison. Neither compiled comparison establishes the borrowing direction. CDIAL 11515 instead concerns the distinct vandur family and explicitly labels Guresi vandur a Lahnda loan; it does not justify assigning these śad-/śod- forms to vānara. |

## Motion and posture verbs held for research

3 cases cover 22 records. These cases have no proposed assignments. Difficulty describes the remaining review; some moderate cases have an explicit source analysis but await an eligible donor or base node.

| Difficulty | Shina | Records | Evidence and next check |
|---|---|---|---|
| Difficult | **buj, boh, bō, bojō̌nu, božṓno** ‘go’ | 6 | CDIAL 12225 subsection 3 cites Shina buž- but explicitly questions the expected development of vr- and offers vañcayati (11208); the reduced bo imperatives cannot settle that choice. Schmidt Kohistani bojō̌nu supplies the fuller infinitive cited under 11208, but does not settle the competing vrájati analysis. Rajapurohit božōno is elicited both for go and leak (item 2165), plausibly an extended motion meaning; this does not settle the competing ancient roots. |
| Difficult | **vayōk, vai, invāh, invas, wa, way, vāʰ, vas, vazō̌nu** ‘come’ | 9 | The wa-/way- series is explicitly assigned competing avāpayati and upayāti origins. The cited past vatu supports comparison with avāpta but does not uniquely resolve the present stem. Further vāh/vas imperatives and Astori vazō̌nu retain the v-initial series; similarity to y-initial walk alone cannot establish identity. |
| Difficult | **bai, baī, byō̌nu, byō̌n, biyō̌nu, byṓno** ‘sit’ | 7 | CDIAL 2245 lists the Shina bay- present and beṭu past together, while explicitly suggesting vasati for the present and warning of contamination and replacement by past-stem forms. The simplex bai imperatives do not resolve that historical analysis. Schmidt Gurezi byō̌nu to sit matches the full infinitive explicitly cited in 2245, but it likewise does not resolve the present-stem versus past-stem analysis. Schmidt Kohistani byō̌n and Astori biyō̌nu likewise preserve the full sitting stem without resolving the competing history. Rajapurohit byōno sit/stay also belongs to the present-stem comparison without resolving the alternative historical analysis. |

| Difficult | **ʦandúro, ʦãdrā̌r, ʦandrā̂lo, ʦádrā̌l, ʦandrā́l** ‘Monday’ | 5 | The shared calendar term needs its historical formation and borrowing route verified; no assignment proposed. |

| Difficult | **bō̂do, bṓdo** ‘Wednesday’ | 2 | The shared calendar term needs its historical formation and borrowing route verified; no assignment proposed. |

Kogan discusses the Burushaski weekday series as possible early Middle Indo-Aryan loans, while retaining very early adapted Sanskrit borrowing as an alternative. This makes shared calendar vocabulary insufficient evidence for a modern Shina donor. The present audit applies that caution to the remaining Shina attestations; it does not claim the paper determines their immediate ancestry. [Source](https://doi.org/10.31826/jlr-2024-221-204).

## Kinship source and etymology checks held for research

7 cases cover 11 records. These cases have no proposed assignments. Difficulty describes the remaining review; some moderate cases have an explicit source analysis but await an eligible donor or base node.

| Difficulty | Shina | Records | Evidence and next check |
|---|---|---|---|
| Difficult | **sazū, sazuy** ‘sister’s son; sister’s daughter’ | 4 | CDIAL 13932 cites Maiya sāzū/sazūi but Shina savuo/savui. The HKAT Palas and Indus Kohistan z-forms resemble the Maiya series; this could reflect regional development or contact, and the entry does not establish a donor for these modern attestations. Do not silently inherit them from the Shina v-series or assign Maiya as donor without further evidence. Turner also prefers *svāsu(k)a over the head svāsṛka. |
| Difficult | **mahul** ‘mother’s brother’ | 1 | CDIAL 10009 gives Shina mōl and a māma-contaminated māmōl, whereas HKAT Palas has mahul. The medial h and vowel sequence need independent dialect evidence before choosing direct inheritance, an alternative formation, or contact. This maternal-uncle form must not be conflated with mahallaka-derived mālo father. |
| Difficult | **māmī** ‘mother’s older brother’ | 1 | HKAT Gilgit māmī is glossed mother’s older brother. The same list uses māmī for mother’s brother’s wife, and CDIAL 10055 places feminine māmī-type formations with the uncle’s wife. Verify the elicitation or gloss; do not silently change its sex or attach the male-uncle history. |
| Difficult | **ʦ̣uṇi mā** ‘father’s older brother’s wife’ | 1 | HKAT Indus Kohistan gives small mother for father’s older brother’s wife, identical to its younger-brother’s-wife response. The lexical components are recognizable, but the age contrast in this specific elicitation remains anomalous. Keep this attestation unassigned pending source review. |
| Difficult | **pʰapeímulə́i** ‘Paternal aunt’s daughter; Paternal uncle’s daughter’ | 1 | Rajapurohit’s phapei-mulai contains the paternal-aunt stem, but the compiled gloss merges paternal aunt’s daughter and paternal uncle’s daughter. The daughter/girl base mulāi is also still unresolved. Verify the merged senses before representing this as a fully resolved aunt-daughter compound. |
| Difficult | **cúṇi āžéu baréu** ‘Maternal aunt’s’ | 1 | The compiled Rajapurohit gloss is truncated to Maternal aunt’s although the form ends in baréu husband. Preserve the existing record and flag the missing relationship head for source correction rather than silently supplying a corrected gloss in an etymology assignment. |
| Difficult | **māmī, mamī** ‘mother’s brother’s wife’ | 2 | The Gilgit and Indus Kohistan feminine māmī/mamī uncle’s-wife forms have close parallels throughout CDIAL 10055, but the entry explicitly cites only masculine Shina māmṷ. A local feminine formation and a regional loan remain possible; a direct ancient reflex should not be inferred solely from the nursery stem’s similarity. |

## Landscape terms held for research

3 cases cover 44 records. These cases have no proposed assignments. Difficulty describes the remaining review; some moderate cases have an explicit source analysis but await an eligible donor or base node.

| Difficulty | Shina | Records | Evidence and next check |
|---|---|---|---|
| Difficult | **hin, hín** ‘snow; Snow’ | 2 | The main sníh entry compares Shina hin snow, but numbered addenda 14840 and 14845 offer *himna instead, with an explicit preference question. The modern forms alone cannot decide between these competing histories; retain both and seek the later discussion before assigning an exact parent. |
| Difficult | **síŋil, síŋel** ‘sand; Sand’ | 5 | Turner’s sikatā entry explicitly discusses Iranian influence on s-initial forms and possible borrowing or suffix variation in the g/k and l/t series. Degener’s T. 13386 citation for sigal supports family identification, but the modern siŋel/siŋil forms add a nasal absent from the cited Shina sigal. Neither the immediate donor nor this nasal development is established; do not flatten the comparison into an unqualified inherited reflex. |
| Difficult | **ōśī, ō̌śī, ō̌̃śī, õõśī, ośī, ōś, ō̌ś, ūśī, ō̂̃śi, ō̂śi, íśi, dos, ṑ̃ši** ‘wind’ | 37 | Degener óo~ši wind cites Atlas 159 and Buddruss 1996 p. 47, without a Turner ancestor. The local Buddruss audit records Phalura and Maiya comparisons but does not reproduce their historical analysis. Neither this metadata nor broad Indo-Aryan wind glosses establishes ancestry. The ōś/ūś series, íśi and dos require separate assessment; do not conflate the noun with the similarly shaped guest word in CDIAL 1446. |

| Moderate | **kam, kamek, kamak, kamakʰ, kamik** ‘few’ | 19 | Degener explicitly derives kamak a little under kam scant/little, labelled a Persian–Urdu loan. The modern ai attestations cannot by themselves select an exact parent, and the clearer Brokskat ā comparison is not evidence that the Shina form has the identical history. Five further female-goat records in Schmidt and LSI are grouped for the same comparison, preserving ā/ai vowel length and nasalization rather than assuming that all are identical phonetic forms. The Rajapurohit glottal-initial ai response is glossed male goat; its sex differs from the female comparison and needs source checking. |

The earlier Gilgit batch supplies no established donor for either form. |
| Difficult | **jek, jō, jok, źok, je, jo, yok, jūk** ‘what’ | 33 | The jek/jok/jō/yok interrogative set requires historical analysis before choosing an Indo-Aryan interrogative or relative parent. That syntactic resemblance does not establish a lexical ancestor for these 33 dialect records; the terminal -k and j/y variation also need explanation. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-70-2009/5342-4682-15579.pdf). Source: Acta Orientalia 70 (2009), book review, p. 238, discussion (2). |
