The primary text, including its exact reconstruction notation, is preserved below. |
| Difficult | **syṓno** ‘Sew’ | 1 | CDIAL 13444 gives Shina sioiki/sīam sew but explicitly permits both sīvyati and sīvayati as its historical source. Rajapurohit syōno agrees with the Shina verb but does not determine the older present-stem formation, so an exact parent selection remains open. |
| Difficult | **bva:no, bayṓno** ‘Weave (cloth); Braid (plait); Dwell (live in); Get well (from illness); Heal; Knit; Weave (cloth)’ | 2 | CDIAL 11300 explicitly marks Shina buyoiki weave, knit, plait as borrowed from Domaaki bu-. No matching Domaaki weaving verb was found in the compiled forms during this check; a direct Sanskrit reflex would skip the stated immediate donor. The Rajapurohit bayōno record additionally combines weaving with dwell, heal and get well, requiring a homonym/source audit before assigning the whole record. The separately transcribed bva:no has the narrower weaving gloss but still lacks a usable donor node. |
| Difficult | **mais** ‘buffalo’ | 1 | CDIAL 9964 attests Shina maiyuṣ, mẽṣ, maĩš and maĩšā in the mahiṣa family. Drasi mais lacks the sibilant distinction and nasal marking of those citations. The lexical comparison is strong but the source notation and possible contact route need checking before assigning a direct reflex. |
| Difficult | **qā̂, koérkuʦ, kórkus, kórkuʦe** ‘crow’ | 4 | CDIAL 2993 gives Shina kā̃/kāh; it does not explain Kohistani qā or Drasi koérkuʦ/kórkus. The longer Drasi noun cannot be assigned wholesale to kāka solely from its crow gloss. These two form types require separate phonological or contact explanations. |
| Difficult | **kā̀̃** ‘caw (of the crow)’ | 1 | Degener’s full primary entry is káa~ káa~ th- caw, but the compiled target is only kā̀̃ and its description also loses one repetition. The source supports a reduplicated sound expression plus th-; assigning ordered components to the shorter compiled form would silently repair source structure. |
| Difficult | **tonḕ** ‘and, also’ | 1 | Degener explicitly segments tonée as to + née, and the separate née entry means again/anew. The available linked nḗ nodes mean not and represent a different accent and lexeme. They must not be reused solely because the normalized spellings resemble one another. |
| Difficult | **lā̂** ‘all (uncountable)’ | 1 | The discovery index’s 9193 match is not supported as a secure Shina etymology. CDIAL bahura lists several neighboring forms in l- but explicitly calls those forms ambiguous, with possible original l-. It does not cite this Guresi lā all noun/adjective. |
| Difficult | **kā̂** ‘what?’ | 1 | The discovery index incorrectly associates Kohistani kā what? with CDIAL 2993 kāka crow, apparently from form resemblance. The gloss makes that match inapplicable. CDIAL 2574 and 3164 are relevant interrogative families, but neither supplies an exact analysis of this Kohistani form. |
| Difficult | **sum, sūm** ‘earth’ | 12 | CDIAL 13493 explicitly calls its derivation of Shina sum earth from sumahant very great very doubtful. The discovery index correctly rejects automatic assignment here. Similarity and gloss agreement with the cited modern forms do not remove Turner’s historical objection. Buddruss sum earth/dust is added as an independent modern attestation; it confirms the word, not the proposed sumahant derivation. Eight sūm dust survey records extend the earth/dust set, retaining their long vowel and dust prompts. |
| Difficult | **béĩ, byõ, bev, byéi** ‘willow’ | 4 | CDIAL 12097 initially distributes Shina beĩ under vēta and byō̃ under *vētuka, but its addendum instead favors *vēti for Dardic and Punjabi. No corresponding reconstructed *vēti head is present in the compiled parent inventory. The modern-form matches do not justify retaining the superseded head assignments. Schmidt–Kaul p. 272, Table 2 items 169 and 171, adds Gilgiti bew and Drasi byéi; the latter is also elicited for generic tree, a scope difference requiring review. The addendum’s reconstructed parent remains unavailable for these records too. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: p. 272, Table 2, items 169, 171. |
| Difficult | **mḗl, méəl** ‘buttermilk’ | 2 | CDIAL 9767 explains Shina mail/mēl as either loans from a dialect with t > l (such as Kalasha) or forms with an -ll- extension. Exact Drasi mēl matches therefore leave inheritance versus borrowing unresolved. |
| Difficult | **sōr, sōər, sōre** ‘ice’ | 3 | CDIAL 13355 labels the sōr ice comparison with sāra solid phonetically doubtful and points to Wakhi sūr. Similarity of the Drasi sōr/sōər forms cannot remove that explicit objection. The further Dras sōre ice record adds an e-ending without settling the phonological and contact problem. |
| Difficult | **tʰúri** ‘heel’ | 3 | CDIAL 13775 explicitly calls the Shina thuri heel derivation from sthūra very doubtful and redirects comparison to *thuḍḍ-. The exact modern-form match alone is insufficient. The merged Schmidt/Rajapurohit Dras thúri heel attestation adds the same lexical evidence but does not strengthen the disputed ancient derivation. |
| Difficult | **nī̂ṣ** ‘sleep’ | 4 | CDIAL 7215 places Shina nī̃š under nipadyate, but explicitly cross-refers to nidrā. CDIAL 7200 discusses competing nidrā, niryā/niryāti and nipadyate analyses and conditions them on the sibilant history. The modern Schmidt nīṣ forms therefore need an actual choice among these analyses; their exact-form index match does not settle the question. |
| Difficult | **gōś** ‘House’ | 1 | LSI Chilas gōś house has palatal ś. CDIAL 4336 treats Shina goṣ as probably from ghōṣa, unless borrowed with loss of ṭ; 4528 explicitly places Shina goṣ/gōṣ under ghōṣa. The source also distinguishes Chilasi got under gōstha. Thus a generic house match to gōṣṭha would suppress both an alternative etymon and the sibilant question. |
| Difficult | **śiṇṭī́li, śaṇṭī́li** ‘Mushroom’ | 2 | CDIAL 12468 only compares Shina šī̃ṭhīli/šĭnṭīli mushroom with śilīndhra; it does not present a straightforward derivation. Dras śiṇṭī́li matches the cited Shina form, but that match does not itself establish the internal consonant development from the proposed ancient head. Rajapurohit śaṇṭīli mushroom adds an initial-a variant of the same unresolved comparison. |
| Difficult | **biẓón** ‘Rainbow’ | 1 | CDIAL 12052 directly cites Shina biẓòn rainbow under reconstructed vīdhradhanus, but immediately offers indradhanus crossed with vīdhra as an alternative. Dras biẓón is an exact lexical match; the competing historical analyses remain unresolved. |
| Difficult | **awā́j** ‘be necessary’ | 1 | Degener explicitly offers CDIAL 1197 or 1221 for awā́j be necessary. Both entries cite Shina avāžoiki and its participle avādu, but compete between āpadyate, ābadhyate and a possible ābādhyate analysis. Degener’s arrow is Shina to Burushaski awāaji, so the Burushaski adjective is not a donor resolving this ambiguity. |
| Difficult | **bac** ‘thunderclap’ | 1 | Degener labels the comparison of bac thunderclap with CDIAL 11204 vajra doubtful. Turner’s Shina bΛc̣ has a retroflex affricate and is itself accompanied by a questioned intermediate form. The modern palatal c and thunderclap meaning cannot simply inherit a confident link from this citation. |
| Difficult | **ḍuk** ‘crashed together’ | 1 | Degener relates ḍuk collided to CDIAL 5557 but that head directly cites Shina ḍukini fighting, with an alternative derivation from ḍhukyati. The short expressive element occurs in ḍuk th- collide or knock eggs together. Its association with a blow word is insufficient to choose inheritance or a particular derivation. |
| Difficult | **tā́o** ‘bread griddle’ | 1 | Degener cites CDIAL 5768 for tā́o bread griddle, comparing Indus Kohistani tʌv and Burushaski dáo. The full CDIAL entry explicitly marks Shina tāo as borrowed from an Indo-Aryan language without specifying which. Neither the comparison nor the remote tāpaka head supplies a verified immediate donor. |
| Difficult | **sī́li** ‘virtuous, virtuous woman’ | 1 | Degener compares sī́li virtuous, virtuous woman with śīlin(ī) under CDIAL 12501 and notes a Burushaski equivalent. CDIAL 12501 is the noun śīla and neither gives the Shina form nor a separate śīlin(ī) node. A direct link to the noun would suppress the specific adjective formation and possible contact history. |
| Difficult | **šenī** ‘garden bed’ | 1 | Degener associates šenī garden bed with CDIAL 12323 and compares Burushaski śaní. Turner directly cites Shina šēn bed, but explicitly calls the garden/pergola comparanda in neighboring languages more doubtful. The final ī and the garden sense need explanation beyond a generic bed match. |
| Difficult | **cukunā̀r** ‘peach tree’ | 1 | Degener gives cukunā̀r peach tree as CDIAL 4850 plus 6298, following Berger. Berger’s Burushaski ćúgudar entry compares Yasin ćuqundár and Shina ćukanáar/ćukunáar with those same heads. This documents the proposed sour-plus-wood analysis, but no borrowing arrow specifies the transmission and the n/r consonant history is not explained. Source: Berger, printed p. 92, ćúgudar. |
| Difficult | **cumuṭkér** ‘girl, young woman’ | 1 | Berger compares Burushaski and Yasin ćumuṭkér with Shina ćumatkér/Lorimer ćumunkiar and Khowar ćumutkēr, proposing approximately *ćuṭakmir from *choṭṭa plus kumārī. Degener cites this analysis. The reconstruction is more specific than two bare CDIAL numbers, but the intermediate is qualified and the comparison does not establish a donor route for Shina. Zoller p. 298 explicitly disputes the two-part *choṭṭa + kumārī account and proposes a three-part duhitṛ + *maṭṭa + kuḍa formation, with an intermediate *čhumaṭukuṛi and vowel shift/metaphony. This competing reconstruction must be assessed alongside Berger; neither donor direction nor ordered components are settled. Source: Berger, printed p. 92, ćéumutkér;zoller2005[p. 298]. |
| Difficult | **áno** ‘millet’ | 1 | Degener tentatively cites CDIAL 192 aṇu for áno millet. Turner directly cites Phalura āṇu, but no Shina form; the retroflex versus dental nasal and the particular grain identification remain to be checked. |
| Difficult | **cḗri** ‘chip (of wood), small piece’ | 1 | Degener tentatively relates cḗri wood chip to CDIAL 4844. Turner cites a Shina splitting verb, but explicitly treats it as borrowed from a dialect with ċ from č. The noun therefore requires both a derivational account and attention to the verb’s contact history; a bare reflex edge to cīrayati would skip those questions. |
| Difficult | **gambéšo** ‘neighbor’ | 1 | Degener tentatively proposes grāmavāsin for gambéšo neighbor. CDIAL 4374 gives the Sanskrit head and Pali/Sinhalese comparanda, with no direct Shina citation. The proposed reduction to gambéšo is not explained by the reference alone. |
| Difficult | **náre** ‘downward; down’ | 1 | Degener cites Buddruss 1996 p. 46 and questions CDIAL 7189 for náre down. Turner independently marks Shina nər(ə) down with a question under nitarām. The close modern match does not remove the uncertainty in the ancient derivation. |
| Difficult | **niš** ‘extinguish (a light)’ | 1 | Degener offers either a relation to niṣādayati with š influenced by Persian nišāndan, or direct borrowing from Persian. CDIAL 7466 does not directly cite Shina and itself flags possible borrowing behind š in Gawri and Khowar. The extinguish meaning adds a semantic step from make sit down. |
| Difficult | **pā́ʦ̣o** ‘piece (of iron)’ | 1 | Degener tentatively compares pā́c̣o piece of iron with pakṣa. CDIAL 7627 has Shina fortnight, tail and wing forms, but no iron-piece sense. The reference alone does not explain the long vowel, ending or specific semantic development. |
| Difficult | **bā̀k d** ‘talk, speak’ | 1 | Degener gives bā̀k d talk/speak with alternatives CDIAL 9117 expressive bakk chatter and 11468 vākya speech. Neither head directly cites this Shina construction, and its light verb must be distinguished from the lexical bāk element. |
| Difficult | **šukā̀ter** ‘dry place’ | 1 | Degener compares šukā̀ter dry place with śuṣka and separately gives šúko dry. This supports a lexical relationship, but the -āter formation needs explanation. CDIAL 12551 śuṣkataṭī dry slope has only a Marathi citation and does not independently justify assigning that compound to Shina. Further checking CDIAL 12550 *śuṣkakāla dry time and 12551 *śuṣkataṭī dry slope found no explicit Shina reflex; these nearby compounds therefore do not resolve the unexplained ending. |
| Difficult | **śtko** ‘dry’ | 1 | Schmidt’s compiled Dras dry record is štko, with the same consonant-only sequence in Original and Phonemic. Neighboring Shina sources attest šuko/šūku under CDIAL 12548, but substituting u for t would be an unverified source correction. |
| Difficult | **mukʰ** ‘face; Edge’ | 1 | The compiled Shina comparandum is placed under mukhya, but CDIAL 10174 explicitly prefers mukha with expressive doubling for the face forms; 10158 cross-refers to that discussion. The Dras record combines Schmidt face with Rajapurohit edge. A simple match to the parsed mukhya parent would miss the preferred analysis and leave the second sense unexamined. |
| Difficult | **rā̀š** ‘king (Dareli for Gilgit Shina ráa)’ | 1 | Degener explicitly labels rā̀š king as Dareli, although the compiled record carries the general Gilgit tag. CDIAL 10679 gives a comparable Gurezi raš, but offers either a Sanskrit loan through *raj or a consonant from oblique forms with jñ. The exact royal title therefore does not resolve the transmission. |
| Difficult | **juk** ‘wood; firewood’ | 1 | Degener juk wood/firewood has no direct etymology. The full CDIAL 5399 addendum compares Shina juk under a third, transitive jhokkati branch with fuel/firewood terms, rather than directly deriving it from the main intransitive jhukkati head. The noun formation and comparison need further support. |
| Difficult | **kʰói, kʰóe** ‘Cap’ | 2 | CDIAL 3942 directly cites Shina khōi cap, but explicitly derives the regional cap words from different Iranian dialects at different stages, with Shina compatible with khōda or khōḍa. The Sanskrit khōla head is not a verified immediate donor. Rajapurohit khōe cap is explicitly paired with khōi in item 627 as its plural; that paradigm does not identify the historical Iranian donor. |
| Difficult | **kui** ‘Village’ | 1 | The exact Shina kui village is independently listed under kuṭī hut and kūpa hollow/valley. CDIAL 3400 includes the semantic range valley, inhabited place, village; 3232 gives village, country. Rajapurohit’s village gloss alone does not choose between those accounts. |
| Difficult | **rīl** ‘Brass’ | 1 | CDIAL 10752 branch 2 directly places Shina rīl brass/bronze/copper under rīrī, but the head also cites an alternative derivation of the Dardic forms from *raktikā in AO xviii p. 248. The modern exact match identifies the lexeme, not the preferred historical reconstruction. |
| Difficult | **nuṣ, nuś, nuš** ‘not; is not’ | 3 | CDIAL 7607 explicitly cites Shina nuš is/am/are not, but tentatively proposes no plus śete. A direct simplex edge to no would leave the final sibilant and possible fused copula unexplained. The modern records also differ between palatal š/ś and retroflex ṣ, and between a general negative gloss and a negative copula. |
| Difficult | **tʰā̌ṇ, tān, tān** ‘cloth’ | 3 | Satpara thāṇ cloth has an explicitly retroflex nasal. CDIAL 6095 places Shina dental thān under *thānna, while its second *thāna branch contains retroflex forms in Sindhi and Marathi. The exact cloth meaning does not establish whether the Satpara nasal reflects a different inherited branch, local change or borrowing. Backstrom’s Gahorabad and Khinar tān cloth have dental nasals but explicitly unaspirated t in their phonemic records, contrasting with Turner’s Shina thān; the missing aspiration needs dialect/source verification before grouping them with the first branch. |
| Difficult | **mōs** ‘Flesh; Month’ | 1 | Rajapurohit’s compiled mōs record combines flesh and month. CDIAL 9982 māṃsa supports the flesh sense, while 10104 māsa supports month, with Shina nasalization varying by dialect. These are distinct historical lexemes despite convergence in the compiled spelling. |
| Difficult | **ḍóri** ‘Ladle’ | 1 | CDIAL 5573 places Shina ḍōri large wooden ladle in an r-extension and explicitly asks whether it is borrowed from Hindi. Rajapurohit ḍóri matches the lexeme, but an inherited edge to the reconstructed spoon head would ignore the possible nearer donor. |
| Difficult | **unyā́l** ‘Thirst; Hungry; Thirst’ | 1 | CDIAL 1927 explicitly treats Shina unyāl hunger/thirst as a derivative of the verbal family represented by unyoiki be hungry. The Rajapurohit record combines thirst with hungry and a thirsty note, mixing noun and adjective uses. It should not simply be assigned as a direct reflex of the ancient finite verb. |
| Difficult | **cē̂i** ‘woman; bird’ | 1 | Schmidt’s compiled Dras cē̂i combines woman (table item 54) with bird (item 128). CDIAL 13736 supports a Shina woman family under *straika/*straikī, whereas bird forms such as cā̃i belong to the caṭaka family in 4571. The shared compiled spelling does not establish one etymon for both senses. |
| Difficult | **ʦ̣ē** ‘Cold; Three; Coldness’ | 1 | The compiled c̣ē combines cold, coldness and three. CDIAL 5994 directly supports the numeral under trayaḥ, but cannot account for the cold senses; those must be checked as separate lexical material. |
| Difficult | **nau** ‘Nine; Drama staging’ | 1 | The compiled nau combines nine with drama staging and preserves two source transcriptions. CDIAL 6984 nava supports the numeral only. A numeral ancestry for the entire record would silently include the unrelated performance sense. |
| Difficult | **boi** ‘Sleeve; Twelve’ | 1 | The compiled boi combines sleeve and twelve. CDIAL 9229 explicitly gives Shina bõi sleeve under bāhu arm, but that does not account for the numeral. Nasalization may also have been lost in the compiled common spelling. |
| Difficult | **paṣ** ‘Fur; Wool; Fortnight’ | 1 | The compiled paṣ combines fur/wool with fortnight. CDIAL 7638 pakṣman cites Shina paṣ wool, with possible Iranian borrowing in this word family; CDIAL 7627 pakṣa cites a distinct Shina pac̣ fortnight. Both a sense merge and the ṣ versus c̣ transcription need resolution. |
| Difficult | **bói** ‘main roof beam’ | 1 | Buddruss bói main roof beam is marked masculine and presents Turner 11182 and 11403 as alternatives. CDIAL 11182 branch 2 gives feminine nasalized bõi under *vaṃśiya; 11403 considers *vaḍikā possible but *vaṃśiya more probable. The preferred comparison is identifiable, but the modern nasalization/gender differences and Buddruss’s fuller reasoning remain to be assessed. |
| Difficult | **sī́** ‘Good’ | 1 | CDIAL 12532 śubha directly cites Shina šo, feminine ši good/better. Rajapurohit sī́ good has dental s in both compiled form and original phonemic notation, not š. It cannot be treated as a mere accent/length spelling variant without checking the sibilant and grammatical form. |
| Difficult | **ʦ̣alṓ** ‘lamp, torch; lamp; torch’ | 2 | Degener explicitly offers CDIAL 8711 or 8766 for c̣alō lamp/torch. Both entries cite the same Shina lighted-torch form; 8711 suggests *pralōka may better explain a and ō, while 8766 records the earlier *prabhāla proposal. The repeated modern attestations do not uniquely choose between the two roots. |
| Difficult | **makái, makáy, makī̂, məkái** ‘maize’ | 6 | Turner places Shina makai maize under markaka, a lexicographic bird name. Witzel’s Harvard manuscript, section 2.4.8 and note 107 (manuscript pp. 15–16), repeats that derivation and includes Shina while discussing older names applied to the recently introduced crop. This supports the published comparison but does not supply a Shina-specific transmission history or explain the bird-to-crop sense transition. Further makay/makī/məkai maize records are included while preserving the vowel and glide differences. [Primary discussion](https://dash.harvard.edu/server/api/core/bitstreams/7312037c-99bf-6bd4-e053-0100007fdf3b/content). Source: Witzel, The Linguistic History of Some Indian Domestic Plants, Harvard manuscript pp. 15–16, section 2.4.8, note 107. |
| Difficult | **mū́lo** ‘Cucumber; Radish’ | 1 | The local Rajapurohit transcription gives mūlo under both cucumber (823) and radish (900). CDIAL 10250 explicitly supports Shina mūlo radish in the mūlaka formation, but not cucumber. This may be a source identification problem or a broader local plant use; an automatic radish link for both would hide that question. |
| Difficult | **bī̃** ‘Seed :]’ | 1 | The compiled seed record has bī̃, but the local drasi transcription leaves item 908 as a damaged Seed label without its form. CDIAL 9250 gives Shina bi/bīh seed, supporting a likely comparison, while the specific nasalized reading and its source recovery remain to be checked. |
| Difficult | **lḗl** ‘visible; known’ | 1 | CDIAL 11122 marks its proposed Shina lēl visible from lokita with a double question mark. Degener also points to Zoller p. 366 and Indus Kohistani lili, comparing Burushaski leél. These comparisons require consultation before treating the highly doubtful ancient derivation as settled. |
| Difficult | **gā́l, gā́le** ‘Wound’ | 2 | CDIAL 4460 explicitly cites Shina gāl wound but suggests borrowing from a dialect with l from t, comparing the history of hundred. Rajapurohit gā́l wound confirms the lexical match; it does not identify that donor or justify skipping it. The further gāle wound plural belongs to the same unresolved donor search. |
| Difficult | **dáṣṭom** ‘grape(s)’ | 1 | Gurezi dáṣṭom grapes is longer than the daṣ form identified as a Kashmiri loan. Its final ṭom suggests a possible tree compound, but the source gloss says grapes and the segmentation has not been established. |
| Difficult | **daś, dáśe** ‘grape(s)’ | 2 | Rajapurohit Dras daś/dáśe grape has palatal ś, whereas Schmidt’s Kashmiri-loan classification concerns daṣ with retroflex ṣ. The singular/plural pair is compatible internally, but its sibilant differs from the classified form. |
| Difficult | **ʦ̣ʰahū̂̃, ʦ̣aū̂̃, ʦ̣avū̂̃** ‘cold’ | 4 | CDIAL 6013 compares Gilgiti c̣ā, Kohistani c̣haũ and Gurezi c̣ăvū̃ with trāsa fear, but explicitly calls the derivation very doubtful and suggests possible comparison with the initial element of Kalasha tramọ̈̄nċ cold/fever. The four Schmidt variants match this Shina set but cannot remove that fundamental uncertainty; no inheritance assignment is proposed. |
| Difficult | **śaŋā̌li, śáŋali, śaŋā́li** ‘chain’ | 4 | CDIAL 12580 identifies Shina śaŋāli as a loan on the evidence of initial ś- from śr̥-, but does not identify an immediate donor. The Schmidt forms match the cited chain word; a direct inherited link to śr̥ṅkhala would conceal the explicitly stated borrowing, so no assignment is made. Two Rajapurohit śaŋali chain records add short and long-vowel variants but do not identify the donor. |
| Difficult | **kʰū̀ṣ** ‘damp’ | 1 | CDIAL 3552 compares Shina kūš damp with *kōṣma warm but explicitly questions the sibilant and proposes a speculative tepid/cool/damp semantic path. Degener khūṣ damp additionally has aspiration; its exact history needs more than this uncertain comparison. |
| Difficult | **tīko** ‘spot, stain’ | 1 | CDIAL 5458 directly records Shina ṭīku spot under *ṭikka. Degener tīko has a dental initial, unlike that retroflex form, and the entry does not supply a cross-reference confirming the change. The apparent match is held for a source and correspondence check. |
| Difficult | **mayaró** ‘A wild animal’ | 1 | CDIAL 10274 explicitly gives Shina maiāro for wild goat/sheep-like animals but offers competing *mr̥gākāra and *mr̥gatara analyses. Rajapurohit mayaró a wild animal belongs plausibly to that lexical set, yet neither the broad gloss nor the form selects between the two historical compounds. |
| Difficult | **sārōṇu̥** ‘wife’s brother’ | 1 | CDIAL 13871 subsection 2 explicitly gives Shina sarōṇi wife’s sister under *syārāṇikā. HKAT Gurez sārōṇu̥ wife’s brother is the masculine counterpart, but linking it directly to the feminine ancient reconstruction would conceal a possible secondary gender formation. A same-language derivation remains a candidate needing morphological evidence; Phalura’s male counterpart has different affinal meanings. |
| Difficult | **barā́o** ‘buckwheat’ | 1 | CDIAL 11313 compares Shina barāo buckwheat with varaṭa safflower seed and explicitly notes the Burushaski counterpart. Degener likewise gives Burushaski and Indus Kohistani comparisons without a borrowing direction. The plant-meaning difference and contact history require checking before treating the ancient safflower word as an inherited parent. Zoller p. 303 reports that Berger questions the varaṭa derivation, adds Tamil varaga as a comparison, and distinguishes the Kohistani millet from Shina/Burushaski buckwheat. This strengthens the need to keep the shared plant name unresolved rather than making an automatic inherited link. Source: ;zoller2005[p. 303]. |
| Difficult | **ẓigá** ‘for a long time’ | 1 | CDIAL 6368 explicitly supports the adjective ẓigu long, but Degener distinguishes adverb ẓigá for a long time, citing Burushaski jigá and Berger 1966 p. 80, from adjective ẓígo with T. 6368. A native adverbial derivation and a contact-related analysis remain possible; the separate citation must be checked before assigning either a direct ancient parent or a modern donor. |
| Difficult | **krĩ́:, krĩ́yẽ, cʰī́mokrĩ́, cʰ** ‘insect, caterpillar, worm; earthworm’ | 4 | CDIAL 3193 explicitly gives Shina krī̃, plural krī̃ye, but leaves initial kr- as either *krīṭa or kīṭa crossed with kr̥mi; no unique parent is selected here. Rajapurohit items 222 and 267 confirm krĩ́: insect, whereas item 327 has damaged worm/plural text. Item 242 earthworm cʰī́mokrĩ́ needs analysis of its first component, and the compiled plural cʰ is visibly truncated, so neither compound nor fragment receives a whole-word reflex link. [Primary discussion](https://www.aa.tufs.ac.jp/~tjun/shina/Grammar_of_Shina_Language_And_Vocabulary.pdf). Source: vocabulary items 222, 242, 267, 327. |
| Difficult | **kóŋo, kaŋī́** ‘comb; source sex-of-user labels’ | 2 | Rajapurohit p. 97 item 634 lists kaŋī for men and kóŋo for ladies; the compiled kóŋo record inherits the men label. CDIAL 2598 includes native Shina kōĩ/kōũ/kōgō but also distinguishes borrowed northwestern comb forms under the *kaṅkaśa branch; kóŋo has a nasal absent in kōgō and kaŋī may be a separate contact form. Resolve the label and phonological/transmission differences before selecting exact ancestry. [Primary discussion](https://www.aa.tufs.ac.jp/~tjun/shina/Grammar_of_Shina_Language_And_Vocabulary.pdf). Source: p. 97, item 634. |
| Difficult | **gat** ‘tent; damaged source text’ | 1 | CDIAL 4202 gupti explicitly cites Lorimer gut tent, but compiled Dras gat has a different vowel and the available Rajapurohit p. 102 item 777 extraction is only a damaged Tent ]) entry with no recoverable response. The match remains unassigned until the original page or another primary attestation verifies gat. [Primary discussion](https://www.aa.tufs.ac.jp/~tjun/shina/Grammar_of_Shina_Language_And_Vocabulary.pdf). Source: p. 102, item 777. |
| Difficult | **darā̀** ‘a tree with reddish hard wood’ | 1 | Degener daráa denotes an unidentified tree with reddish hard wood and only compares CDIAL 6298. Turner’s dāru wood/tree and dāruka branches have no Shina example here; the final long ā, accent and botanical specialization need an account before selecting a branch or treating the word as a direct simplex reflex. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 259, glossary entry 18]. |
| Difficult | **duṣṭanī̀** ‘envy, ill will’ | 1 | Degener explicitly writes T. 6472 + ? for duṣṭanī envy/ill will. CDIAL duṣṭa bad supplies only the possible first element, with no Shina attestation or explanation of -anī; a whole-word reflex would hide the unresolved formation. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 262, glossary entry 14]. |
| Difficult | **ragṑṭo, ragoṭyā́r** ‘sick, sick person; illness’ | 2 | Degener compares Burushaski rugóoṭo and Berger’s alternatives CDIAL 10759–60 for ragōṭo sick. Berger p. 366 reciprocally compares Shina ragóoto without a directional borrowing statement; the compiled Burushaski candidate f_y3n3q5rz6lows is unlinked and its unusual ṭļ sequence is from unreviewed OCR. Neither contact direction nor the extended Shina formation is established. Berger p. 366 additionally records the Burushaski abstract rugutiār illness derived from rugōṭo, so Degener’s Shina ragoṭyār cannot be assigned as an independent Shina derivation without resolving contact direction; include it in the same held family. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 296, glossary entry 12]. |
| Difficult | **šon** ‘penis’ | 1 | Degener links šon penis only generally to CDIAL 12270. That entry has several defective-word branches with divergent stops and nasal clusters, and no matching Shina penis citation; the semantic comparison to impotent/eunuch does not select a unique parent. This noun must also remain distinct from šóno barren, which has a different supported comparison. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 303, glossary entry 3]. |
| Difficult | **d** ‘give; throw, hit’ | 2 | Degener’s single d- entry combines give with throw/hit and cites both 6141 dadāti and 6145 dadhāti. Turner explicitly discusses phonetic and semantic collision of the two verbs in Dardic and assigns Shina giving versus putting/building/beating to the respective entries; one parent for the entire merged modern record would conceal that historical convergence. Buddruss’s separate d give/strike/throw record has the same conflated source references and is grouped here for the same review. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 258, glossary entry 12]. |
| Difficult | **badéi** ‘misfortune, badness; captivity’ | 1 | Degener separates badéi misfortune/badness, tentatively Persian–Urdu badī, from captivity, tentatively baddha plus -i. CDIAL 9126 supports Shina bado prisoner, not a unitary origin for both sense groups; the record requires a homonym/formation review before choosing an immediate donor or inherited base. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 247, glossary entry 11]. |
| Difficult | **carók** ‘anus, arse’ | 1 | Turner 4538 relates Shina curūk/corōk buttocks to cakra wheel, but Degener explicitly doubts this comparison for carók anus/arse and instead compares Burushaski čarák carcass/hindquarters. The resemblance does not override this primary-source disagreement or establish a borrowing direction. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 254, glossary entry 22]. |
| Difficult | **hī̀ji tʰ** ‘think of’ | 1 | Degener only tentatively compares the expression hīji th- think of with CDIAL 14154 hṛdya. Turner actually cites Shina hižu own/full (of siblings), so the different first-member shape and semantic route need explanation; th- is a separate light verb and the whole expression cannot be assigned directly to hṛdya. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 270, glossary entry 1]. |
| Difficult | **hū̀n tʰ** ‘pick up; lift up’ | 1 | Degener cites Buddruss’s question-marked comparison of hūn th- lift up with ūrdhva. CDIAL 2426 has Phalura huṇḍ/huṇṭe above and many raised/up forms but no direct Shina hūn citation; the first member and the light verb must be analysed separately, with the proposed consonant development still unresolved. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 270, glossary entry 16]. |
| Difficult | **wal** ‘fetch, bring; fetch; bring’ | 2 | Degener and Buddruss both question the connection of transitive wal- fetch/bring with CDIAL 14246. Turner places Kohistani valōnu and Gurezi valyōnu descend/fall in the intransitive *avakalati branch, while the causative *avakālayati branch cites Kashmiri bring down; the modern Gilgit transitive stem needs a historical valency account before choosing either branch. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 312, glossary entry 17]; buddruss-shina1996[p. 50, glossary entry 02]. |
| Difficult | **mūlāī, mūlāi, mūlai, mulái, mulay, mulā̀i** ‘girl; daughter’ | 20 | Degener refers muláai girl to Zoller, whose p. 341 Gowro mulʌ́y entry explicitly compares Shina muláay and proposes *mūlā-laṭī, a near-synonym compound interpreted as rascal girl (cf. CDIAL 10254, 10924). Those Turner entries supply a lunar-mansion/orphan/rascal complex and *laḍikka child, not the exact reconstructed compound or its *laṭī member; retain Zoller’s proposal as a difficult lead pending sound-development and graph-representation checks, rather than substituting the unrelated root/radish entry 10250. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 286, glossary entry 14];zoller2005[p. 341, mulʌ́y];CDIAL[10254, 10924]. |
| Difficult | **kaṣā́p** ‘violent jerky movement; tugging at something; flailing about’ | 1 | Zoller p. 125 explicitly compares Shina kaṣáp jerky grabbing movement and suggests a compound of a quick/sudden base (with competing *khass comparison) and *carpa palm of hand. The proposed first element is not fixed and the historical compound is not represented by exact existing nodes; preserve this lead without confusing it with the homonymous bird name. Source: zoller2005[p. 125]. |
| Difficult | **budúl** ‘rag’ | 1 | Zoller p. 315 explicitly compares Shina/Burushaski budúl worn-out clothes and proposes a probable synonym compound of *bodda and dala. This tentative historical decomposition is useful evidence, but it does not establish the immediate route into Shina or justify replacing the whole word with one component; the compound and contact history require review. Source: zoller2005[p. 315]. |
| Difficult | **śadī, śodī, śodo, śadī́, śādo, śadyḗ** ‘monkey’ | 27 | Zoller p. 382 compares Shina śódo, Burushaski śadí and Pashto šādo with Persian šādī ape. Berger p. 388 also records the Shina masculine/feminine pair śōdo/śōdi, so the i/o alternation need not identify a donor language; the available comparisons establish a contact family but not a unique immediate transmission route. Keep the Shina records open instead of assigning all i-forms to Burushaski or all o-forms to Pashto. Source: zoller2005[p. 382, šādū];berger1998[vol. 3, p. 388, śadí]. |
| Difficult | **cāṭal, caṭal, caṭāl, caṭál, cātal, catal** ‘axe’ | 21 | Zoller p. 182 places Shina caṭal axe beside Burushaski and Domaaki and the Kohistani small axe, but supplies no direction of borrowing; Degener likewise gives an equality comparison. The forms support a shared contact set, while the proposed relationship to other Kohistani items is only a cross-reference; do not infer a particular donor from resemblance. Source: zoller2005[p. 182]. |
| Difficult | **ginṭí, gintí** ‘mattock (hoe)’ | 2 | Zoller p. 148 compares Shina giṇti pickaxe with Kohistani, Burushaski, Hindi geṁtī/gaiṁtī and a tentative Yidgha parallel. The modern Hindi form is a possible donor lead, but this entry does not select a transmission route or account for the Shina nasal/place variants, so the two mattock records remain unresolved. Source: zoller2005[p. 148]. |
| Difficult | **ú̃co** ‘fire tongs’ | 1 | Degener compares the Shina fire-tongs term with Burushaski učo, and Zoller p. 82 adds Kohistani pincers and Pashto nūċaī. These are lexical comparisons rather than an explicit loan-direction statement; the Shina initial nasalization and the competing contact routes need checking before choosing a donor. Source: zoller2005[p. 82]. |
| Difficult | **muʦ̣ʰṓ** ‘(to the) front’ | 1 | Zoller’s mṹĩṭhʌṽ discussion analyzes Shina muc̣hō in front as *muṭ plus ẓhō from, parallel to a Kohistani postpositional expression, rather than a direct equivalent of the bare Kohistani stem; he reconstructs *mukhasthā for that wider base. Exact Shina component nodes with these meanings were not found: the existing muṭ/muṭh nodes mean fist, arm or shoulder and must not be substituted. Degener’s explicit Zoller cross-reference therefore remains a morphological research lead. Source: degener-shina2008[muʦ̣ʰṓ];zoller2005[pp. 342–343, mṹĩṭhʌṽ]. |
| Difficult | **guṭúmo, guṛúmo** ‘deep; Deep’ | 2 | Degener refers guṭúmo deep to Zoller p. 144, whose comparison includes Kohistani, Burushaski, Domaaki and Khowar and only tentatively suggests a Dravidian connection with Tamil kuṭṭam depth/pond. This neither establishes an immediate donor nor a regular derivation; Rajapurohit’s guṛúmo additionally has a flap in place of the stop, so both source forms remain visible in the unresolved set. Source: zoller2005[p. 144, guṭùm];degener-shina2008[p. 267, guṭúmo]. |
| Difficult | **guṣpúr** ‘barn for straw’ | 1 | Degener and Zoller compare Shina guṣpúr straw barn with Burushaski ġurpúṣ and Kohistani ghuṣíli. Zoller cross-refers to gōṣuíli cowshed, separately reconstructed as *gośraya-kula, but does not demonstrate that ordered derivation for Shina’s metathesized-looking form or specify a borrowing route; retain the reconstruction as a lead rather than assigning its two remote components. Source: zoller2005[pp. 148, 155, gōṣuíli and ghuṣíli];degener-shina2008[p. 267, guṣpúr]. |
| Difficult | **cā́k** ‘bite’ | 1 | Degener’s čaák is explicitly a feminine noun meaning bite, compared tentatively with *cakṣati tastes and Burushaski čaq chew. Turner 4557 has no Shina citation and the available reflexes normally retain aspiration or a fricative/affricate outcome; the nominal formation, unaspirated final k and contact direction need an account before selecting an inherited verb parent. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 254, glossary entry 4];CDIAL[4557]. |
| Difficult | **koṣ** ‘detach, remove (meat from the bones)’ | 1 | Degener tentatively compares koṣ- detach/remove meat from bones with kárṣati draws/pulls. The full CDIAL entry has no Shina attestation; its other Dardic pull stems support a semantic lead but do not by themselves explain the Shina o-vowel or establish this specific derivation, so the queried assignment remains open. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 277, glossary entry 12];CDIAL[2908]. |
| Difficult | **ẓḗk tʰ** ‘stretch out’ | 1 | Degener compares the light-verb expression ẓēk th- stretch out with dairgha/dairghya length and Burushaski jeék. Turner’s relevant comparison is Phalura drhēg dūm, literally place my length, but he supplies no Shina form; both the history of the nonverbal element and the separate do-verb need representation, rather than treating the complete phrase as an inherited reflex of length. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 315, glossary entry 4];CDIAL[6568]. |
| Difficult | **jal b** ‘be dishevelled (of hair)’ | 1 | Degener tentatively connects jal b be dishevelled (hair) with jal net. While the main CDIAL 5213 notes Shina’s unexplained short a, separate addendum 14519 explicitly identifies the Shina net word as a Khowar loan. Preserve that donor requirement as well as the tentative hair metaphor and light verb; the whole expression is not a direct reflex of jāla. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 271, glossary entry 18];CDIAL[5213, 14519]. |
| Difficult | **kā́̃ri** ‘coal’ | 1 | Degener explicitly leaves alternatives for feminine kā̃ri coal: *kāra live coal, possibly with influence of aṅgāryā embers, or *skāra. The first has exact Shina masculine kā̃ru charcoal, while 13649 cites aspirated Palas/Jijali forms and 131 has no Shina attestation; a simple feminine formation is plausible, but the source’s alternative contamination analysis remains unresolved. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 275, glossary entry 12];CDIAL[3055, 131, 13649]. |
| Difficult | **wárku** ‘entrails’ | 1 | Degener compares warku entrails with Burushaski wark and udára belly plus an unidentified element. CDIAL 1932 explicitly records Shina ūar belly, but does not explain the k-extension or specify transmission between Shina and Burushaski; neither a direct whole-word reflex nor a donor link is yet established. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 312, glossary entry 19];CDIAL[1932]. |
| Difficult | **wa** ‘come’ | 1 | The full CDIAL entries explicitly cross-reference competing origins for Shina vayóiki/vām come: avāpayati reaches and upayāti approaches. The preterite vatu supports avāpta, while the present/future stem can be assigned either way in Turner; Degener preserves both references and wa-/wai- stems, so a single unqualified parent would conceal the stated alternative. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 312, glossary entry 11];CDIAL[884, 2207]. |
| Difficult | **buj** ‘go; hence secondarily also: become’ | 1 | Degener’s buj- go/become entry includes present/future buj-, imperative bo and suppletive ge-/gā- past forms. Turner gives gata (4008) for the past but explicitly leaves the present between *vrajyati (12225, subsection 3) and vañcati/vañcayati (11208), noting that inherited vr- normally gives Shina ẓ-, not b-; one unqualified parent for the merged paradigm would hide both suppletion and that sound-law problem. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 252, glossary entry 19];CDIAL[12225, 4008, 11208]. |
| Difficult | **šákar** ‘sugar’ | 1 | Degener explicitly leaves šakar sugar between CDIAL 12338 and Urdu borrowing. Turner gives Gilgit šakar and Kohistani šakhar and proposes a Northwest Prakrit background also connected with the Persian loan; phonetic resemblance alone therefore does not distinguish inheritance from renewed contact. Preserve the two routes until a dialect-specific historical or borrowing statement resolves them. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 301, glossary entry 13];CDIAL[12338]. |
| Difficult | **śélśəkə́r** ‘Sugar candy’ | 1 | Rajapurohit p. 96 item 601 prints two words, śél śəkar sugar candy, although the compiled form is joined. Tibetan shel ka ra crystallized sugar supplies a plausible contact comparison, but the Shina second word includes ś- and matches the separate Indo-Aryan/Iranian sugar family; neither a simple whole-word Tibetan borrowing nor a śaila-stone compound is established, so both segmentation and transmission require review. [Primary discussion](https://www.aa.tufs.ac.jp/~tjun/shina/Grammar_of_Shina_Language_And_Vocabulary.pdf). Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 96, item 601]. |
| Difficult | **dáśooi, dáśo, oi** ‘wine; source component glosses’ | 3 | Rajapurohit p. 96 item 610 explicitly decomposes dáśo oi wine as grapes + water. The compiled dáśo Wine and oi Water; Wine records include explanatory component material, while the whole dáśooi is joined; preserve these source-gloss problems and the clear segmentation, but leave ancestry pending because the palatal grape stem’s relationship to the classified Kashmiri loan daṣ remains unresolved. [Primary discussion](https://www.aa.tufs.ac.jp/~tjun/shina/Grammar_of_Shina_Language_And_Vocabulary.pdf). Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 96, item 610]. |
| Difficult | **kṓ̃ tʰ, ṭṓ̃-kṓ̃ tʰ** ‘moo; bellow like a bull’ | 2 | Degener identifies ṭõ-kõ th- bellow as an echo formation from kõ, and separately compares kõ th- moo with Burushaski qoṅ. The expressive relationship is explicit, but the comparison does not establish a borrowing direction or a supported ancestry for the sound base; retain both formations for review, excluding the homonymous kõ shoulder blade. Source: degener-shina2008[pp. 276, 310, glossary entries 14, 7]. |
| Difficult | **ōlo, bōlo** ‘burden, load; echo word’ | 2 | Degener gives ōlo burden, load and explicitly calls bōlo its echo word. This supports the synchronic echo relationship, but no deeper ancestry is supplied for ōlo and its record is unlinked; do not assign bōlo an independent inherited etymon or conflate ōlo with a similar-sounding homonym. Source: degener-shina2008[pp. 252, 290, glossary entries 4, 1]. |
| Difficult | **bazanō̌** ‘spring’ | 1 | Astori bazanō spring differs from the bazōnu/bazōno series by its vowel pattern and final stress. The article’s retrieved table also renders bazanoo, so this is not silently normalized to the neighboring dialect forms; verify the printed vowels and a historical explanation before extending CDIAL 11439. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: schmidt[p. 267, items 108–109];CDIAL[2144];CDIAL[11439]. |
| Difficult | **uzālo, bazoéno** ‘spring; summer’ | 2 | The Dras article entries give uzālo for spring and bazoéno for summer, whereas CDIAL 2144 *upakāla supports Shina uvālu summer and CDIAL 11439 vasanta supports bazōdu/bazōnu spring. Seasonal meaning shifts are possible, but uzālo also has an unexplained z and the compiled summer vowel sequence differs from the table rendering; preserve both source/transcription and semantic questions before assigning either root. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: schmidt[p. 267, items 108–109];CDIAL[2144];CDIAL[11439]. |
| Difficult | **surē̂n, surē̂ṇ, suryōn** ‘sunny side of mountain’ | 5 | Schmidt and Kaul p. 267 item 105 give surēn/surēṇ and Dras suryōn sunny side of mountain. CDIAL 13574 subsection 3 explicitly supports Shina sūri sun/sunshine, but does not supply the extended slope formation; the parallel Dras shady-side term suggests further morphology without identifying it, so neither an inherited whole-word reflex nor a specific suffix etymology is established. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: schmidt[p. 267, items 105–106];CDIAL[13574, subsection 3]. |
| Difficult | **cijō̌ṭ** ‘shade; shady side of mountain’ | 2 | Schmidt’s cijoṭ shady side corresponds closely to the chijoṭ shade family cited by Degener and Buddruss under CDIAL 5027. Turner’s final note says the Shina form is scarcely from *chiyā equated with Greek skia; inclusion under the chāyā entry is not an unqualified derivation statement, so the exact historical analysis and the unaspirated source spelling require review. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: schmidt[p. 267, item 106];rajapurohit2012[p. 79, item 62];CDIAL[5027]. |
| Difficult | **chóri, churyéon, cʰóro** ‘shade; shady side of mountain’ | 4 | Schmidt’s chori shady side and Dras churyéon correspond semantically to Rajapurohit’s choro shade, but CDIAL 5027 does not explicitly derive these r-bearing Shina forms. Keep the simple shade words and the extended Dras slope formation distinguishable; resemblance to the chāyā family and parallelism with suryōn sunny slope do not establish the consonant development or the ending. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: schmidt[p. 267, item 106];rajapurohit2012[p. 79, item 62];CDIAL[5027]. |
| Difficult | **likʰō̌k, likō̌n, likʰyō̌nu, likʰō̌nu, likʰyṓno** ‘write; take down’ | 6 | CDIAL 11048 distinguishes likhati scratches/writes, with MIA lihaï and later lih- forms, from addendum subsection 2 likhyatē, associated with Punjabi, Bengali and Hindi writing verbs. Schmidt’s five Shina dialect forms and Rajapurohit Dras likhyōno have no explicit Shina attribution in this entry; retention of kh and dialectal k, plus the y-bearing infinitives, require a historical or contact account before choosing inheritance, passive-based remodeling or an immediate donor. [Primary discussion](https://www.wisdomlib.org/uploads/journals/acta-orie/vol-69-2008/7372-6759-23258.pdf). Source: CDIAL[11048, main entry and addendum];schmidt[p. 282, item 297];rajapurohit2012. |
| Difficult | **šišerṓk** ‘lung disease; tuberculosis’ | 1 | Degener reports Buddruss’s analysis śuṣya dry + rōg illness. The second member has an existing Shina node, but the first is a reconstructed form rather than the ordinary šuko dry adjective; substituting the linked dry-up verb or the adjective would erase that analysis, so exact first-component identity and compound history remain for review. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 302, glossary entry 22]. |
| Difficult | **śuśryṓno** ‘dry in the sun’ | 1 | Rajapurohit p. 146 item 2028 presents śuśryōno as a variant of śuśyōno, but the additional r may represent causative morphology; no transitive/intransitive label is supplied there. The plain stem is proposed under CDIAL 12559 in proposal 468, while this variant needs a valency and formation check. Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 146, item 2028];CDIAL[12559]. |
| Difficult | **kʰaj, kʰajij** ‘scratch; itch’ | 2 | Degener cross-refers khaj- scratch/itch to khan- scratch and labels khajij- itch passive, while separately attesting the ordinary khanij- passive already in the review. CDIAL 2689 supports the khan family and discusses contamination behind kh-, but does not explain n/j alternation; keep this related pair separate until its stem formation is established rather than treating both as ordinary inflections. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 279, glossary entry 8];degener-shina2008[p. 279, glossary entry 9];CDIAL[2689]. |
| Difficult | **dā́pʰar, pʰar** ‘turn; turn around/inside out’ | 2 | Degener links dāphar- to phar-, but explicitly questions whether intransitive phar-/pher- are still distinguished and attests the transitive phar- only through an absolutive and the prefixed verb. CDIAL 9050 *pharati and 9078 *phirati/*phērayati distinguish overlapping turning families and valencies; choosing a stem, and explaining dā-, requires more than substituting the linked intransitive homonym. Source: degener-shina2008[pp. 259, 293];CDIAL[9050];CDIAL[9078]. |
| Difficult | **kuṭ** ‘thunder’ | 1 | Degener p. 278 actually prints kuṭ dij- thunder, illustrated by hagāi-kuṭ dij-, and connects kuṭ to the noun wall. The compiled record omits dij-; assigning bare kuṭ as a derived thunder verb or to the homonymous beat/thresh root would misrepresent the source. Review the missing light verb and phrase representation before linking this record; the separate sky-wall compound is already proposal 429. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 278, glossary entry 14]. |
| Difficult | **ḍir b** ‘be shoved down; roll on the ground; sacrifice oneself’ | 1 | Degener explicitly marks borrowing from Burushaski ḍir man- and describes the base as a diminutive of ḍar. Berger p. 131 confirms a dar/dir rolling-flowing family and the dir man predicate, but the compiled donor dir has lost the retroflex distinction and the Shina expression substitutes b-; review donor transcription and the borrowed base plus Shina light verb rather than assigning the whole phrase to an unrelated dir homonym. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 263, glossary entry 7];berger-auto[p. 69 (printed p. 131)]. |
| Difficult | **pasun, pasunij** ‘save; be thrifty’ | 2 | Degener gives pasun- save/be thrifty with imperatives pasunyō and pasuné, and explicitly derives pasunij- with imperative pasunīj from it. The local relationship is supported, but neither entry supplies a deeper etymology and no corroborating primary comparison was located; retain the derivation for review without inventing an inherited root or donor for pasun-. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 291, glossary entry 12];degener-shina2008[p. 291, glossary entry 13]. |
| Difficult | **bīpʰao** ‘sowing; sowing festival’ | 1 | Degener explicitly segments bī-phao and compares Burushaski pháo scatter/sow and Indus Kohistani phav karav; Zoller p. 290 confirms the same Shina comparison but gives no direction. The seed component is already approved under CDIAL 9250; no independent verified Shina phao node or donor route is available, and CDIAL 9257 bījavāpa cites only Lahnda, so it cannot substitute for the unresolved compound history. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 251, glossary entry 16];zoller2005[p. 290];CDIAL[9250];CDIAL[9257]. |
| Difficult | **šugúli, šugúlo** ‘female friend; friend’ | 2 | Degener lists šuguli female friend beside šugulo friend with = Burushaski, and Berger p. 398 likewise records śugulo friend/lover and feminine śuguli with Shina comparisons. The gender pair is real in both languages, but neither comparison establishes direction; do not infer either a whole-word borrowing or an independent Shina feminine derivation from resemblance alone. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 303, glossary entry 12];degener-shina2008[p. 303, glossary entry 13]. |
| Difficult | **kʰirí, kʰirī́no, kʰírō, kʰarī, kʰīrī, kʰirī, kʰere, kʰare, kʰire, kʰāre, kʰārī** ‘below; located below; from below’ | 31 | Degener distinguishes khiri below, the adjective khirīno (feminine khirīni) located below, and khiro from below, also used as a postposition. This supports a local spatial family and an adjectival formation parallel to ajīno above, but no verified deeper ancestry for khiri or grammatical account of the source-direction ending was located; preserve the three functions and exclude similarly spelled scratch/roughness roots. The survey adds khiri/khari/khere forms for below across dialects; preserve their vowel and ending differences as comparative attestations of the spatial family, not as evidence for a new Sanskrit root. The separate khunā response is excluded. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 280, glossary entry 12];degener-shina2008[p. 280, glossary entry 14];degener-shina2008[p. 280, glossary entry 15];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **arī́no, arú, darú** ‘inner; inside; into’ | 3 | Degener distinguishes arú inside, arīno inner/own, and darú into explicitly segmented da arú, with variant de arú. CDIAL 357 lists Shina dar/dura inside under antara, but does not explain the bare arú or this source’s segmentation; the separately linked darú outside belongs to another entry, so do not use it as a parent merely because the surface forms coincide. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 245, glossary entry 4];degener-shina2008[p. 245, glossary entry 7];degener-shina2008[p. 260, glossary entry 3]. |
| Difficult | **darú** ‘have diarrhea’ | 1 | Degener actually prints darú buj- have diarrhea, literally go out; the compiled target retains only darú. Restore or review the phrase representation before assigning components, and keep it distinct from both the ordinary outside adverb and the separate da arú into expression. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 260, glossary entry 2]. |
| Difficult | **garī̀b, garibī** ‘poor; poverty’ | 2 | Degener explicitly derives unaccented garibī poverty from garīb poor plus abstract -ī, but separately questions whether stressed garībī reflects renewed Urdu borrowing or a learned pronunciation. The adjective is itself marked Urdu-mediated borrowing and remains without a verified donor link; preserve the ordinary local abstract versus the separately attested learned-looking variant, rather than assigning all tokens to one route. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 265, glossary entry 11];degener-shina2008[p. 265, glossary entry 12]. |
| Difficult | **jin-balā̀** ‘spirits’ | 1 | Degener attests only plural jin-balāi and explicitly proposes reinterpretation of final -ī in borrowed balāī as a Shina plural ending, with an apparent association to balā misfortune. Treat the printed singular head as an analytical back-formation, not independently attested speech; immediate donor nodes and the reanalysis need review before assigning compound components. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 273, glossary entry 2]. |
| Difficult | **yarā̀nak** ‘friend’ | 1 | Degener explicitly relates yarānak friend to Persian yār and its plural yārān. Preserve the plural stem and final -ak rather than assigning a simple direct loan from singular yār; no verified immediate donor node for the relevant form was found. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 313, glossary entry 20]. |
| Difficult | **ḍṑki** ‘dimple’ | 1 | Degener relates feminine ḍōki dimple to Burushaski–Shina ḍōko small grain stack or small pond. The local semantic and morphological comparison is useful, but the base has no verified node and the source does not establish borrowing direction; unrelated ḍok meet is not an eligible substitute. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 263, glossary entry 9]. |
| Difficult | **kurtáni** ‘shirt’ | 1 | Degener tentatively segments kurtáni shirt as Persian–Urdu kurta plus possibly tani band, marking the second component with two question marks. Preserve this uncertainty and do not claim an established compound or skip to a remote ancestor; verified immediate donor/component nodes remain unavailable. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 278, glossary entry 5]. |
| Difficult | **hifā́ b** ‘be sad’ | 1 | Degener analyzes hifā b be sad as Urdu-mediated Arabic ḥaif with Shina adverbial -ā and a light verb. The isolated hifā sorrow token is itself probably shortened from the infinitival expression; preserve the phrase and local morphology, while the required immediate donor remains unverified. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 269, glossary entry 22]. |
| Difficult | **kʰḕr ge** ‘well’ | 1 | Degener explicitly describes Urdu-modeled khēr ge well: borrowed khair plus Shina instrumental ge with/through, modeled on Urdu khair se. The local postposition is available (f_pibunnfndjlai), but the immediate donor for khair is not; the similar Indo-Aryan acacia word is unrelated. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 280, glossary entry 9]. |
| Difficult | **pʰupúṣ** ‘fireplace with two stones’ | 1 | Degener proposes Burushaski phu fire as the first part of phupuṣ fireplace with two stones but leaves the second part explicitly unknown. Burushaski fire attestations exist, yet this does not establish the complete formation or permit treating the whole word as a direct loan from phu. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 295, glossary entry 18]. |
| Difficult | **mukʰ d** ‘turn toward (someone)’ | 1 | Degener prints mukh d turn toward someone with a dative object, directly after mukh face. The first component is secure, but the source combines give and throw/hit under d and cites CDIAL 6141 and 6145; that unresolved base lacks an eligible ancestry link. Preserve this phrase for component review instead of silently choosing one historical verb. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 286, glossary entry 12]. |
| Difficult | **pʰaṛṓro, pʰaṛōryā́r** ‘Thick; Thickness’ | 2 | Rajapurohit p. 114 items 1158–1159 explicitly pairs phaṛōro thick with phaṛōryār thickness, supporting a local abstract in -yār. The base has no verified deeper ancestry or eligible linked node; a generic thick/fat comparison with sthūla does not explain its phaṛōr- stem. Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 114, items 1158–1159]. |
| Difficult | **ḍáŋo, ḍaŋyā́r** ‘Tall; High; Long; Length’ | 2 | Rajapurohit gives ḍaŋo tall/high/long and ḍaŋyār length, a transparent local abstract relationship. Similar CDIAL ḍaṅg- stems occur in separate hill, stick, defective and boat families (5423, 5520, 5524, 5568); none supplies verified Shina adjective evidence, so do not pick a homophonous reconstruction to make the derivation eligible. Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 112, items 1084, 1097, p. 116, item 1193]. |
| Difficult | **bostī́no** ‘Wide’ | 1 | Rajapurohit p. 115 item 1178 prints bostīno wide, whereas broad at p. 110 item 1040 is bistīṇi. The latter has a close Palula counterpart under CDIAL 12009, but this o-vowel and nonretroflex n in the wide form need separate dialectal or transcription evidence before grouping them as mere gender inflections. Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 115, item 1178, p. 110, item 1040]. |
| Difficult | **huḍor, oṛor̃, oṛor, ḍoro, ḍorī, doro, hūḍarī, hiḍor̃, hiḍorī, uḍṑr** ‘mortar’ | 26 | Degener attests uḍōr mortar without an etymological citation; the Backstrom list supplies related-looking huḍor, oṛor, hūḍarī and shorter ḍoro/doro forms whose relationships still need confirmation. CDIAL 2360 distinguishes ulūkhala, ulukkhala, udūkhala and udukkhala, but gives no Shina form or demonstrated development to this retroflex/r-stem. Do not choose a subsection from the gloss alone, and exclude the separate uḍor search verb and ḍōri ladle; the laŋgrī and heroṛ responses are left outside this comparison. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 311, glossary entry 11];backstrom1992;CDIAL[2360]. |
| Difficult | **ōśī, ō̌śī, ō̌̃śī, õõśī, ośī, ōś, ō̌ś, ūśī, ṑ̃ši** ‘wind’ | 29 | Zoller p. 87 ūìši wind compares Shina ṍõši/óoši, Palula hūši and Savi hūši, with Atlas 159, but offers no deeper etymology. This supports the comparative set and its h-/zero and nasal variants, not an automatic direct link to vāta or vāyu; retain the Backstrom pitch and nasalization distinctions. Source: zoller2005[p. 87];degener-shina2008[p. 290, glossary entry 4];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **cʰīmo, cʰūmo, cʰūmū, cimo, cʰimo, cūmo, cīmo, cūbo, śībo, cʰibo, cʰibū, śibo, cibū** ‘fish’ | 28 | Zoller p. 173 ċhīm fish compares Shina/Burushaski čhímo, čhúmo and Tamil campai (DED 1940), without establishing a Tamil loan route. Schmidt–Kaul p. 245 explicitly classify Kohistani chubo as borrowed from Burushaski; apply that claim only to the relevant dialect, while the other Shina variants and deeper ancestry remain unresolved. Degener and Berger p. 101 corroborate the forms, but eligible donor ancestry and the Backstrom m/b, i/u and aspiration variants still need review. Source: zoller2005[p. 173];degener-shina2008[p. 257, glossary entry 2];berger-auto[p. 101];backstrom1992;schmidt[p. 245]. |
| Difficult | **hásto, hástodon** ‘elephant; tusk; ivory’ | 2 | Rajapurohit p. 136 item 1781 explicitly glosses hasto don ivory as elephant’s tooth; p. 85 item 244 independently gives hasto elephant, while p. 87 item 320 labels hasto tusk. CDIAL 14039 treats Shina hasto as possibly an early hastin loan remodeled with a masculine ending, possibly via Kashmiri host, rather than securely inherited hastaka; retained st is irregular. The compound is transparent, but the eligible immediate elephant parent and its borrowing route remain unresolved, and the tusk elicitation must be preserved. Source: rajapurohit2012[p. 85, item 244, p. 87, item 320, p. 136, item 1781];CDIAL[14025, 14039]. |
| Difficult | **ráðośā́, rá** ‘boiled vegetable; boiled rice’ | 2 | Rajapurohit p. 93 item 514 prints ráðo śā boiled vegetable, and pp. 35–36 confirm the dental fricative as an alternative to d in this cooked-word family (including rādek/ráðek in boiled rice). The vegetable component is now supported independently; cooked can be compared with Turner’s Shina radu under the raddha branch, but no independent Dras cooked participle node is available. The compiled rice head rá is truncated relative to the longer grammatical example and needs transcription review before assigning the complete expression. Source: rajapurohit2012[pp. 35–36, p. 93, items 513–514]. |
| Difficult | **pī̂ẓo** ‘flea’ | 1 | Schmidt’s further pīẓo flea attestation resembles the Kohistani p-form in CDIAL 9029-5. Separate addendum 14711, however, argues that Shina prī̃ẓu is probably borrowed from Palula prī̃ṣu because inherited pr normally became c̣; the proposed donor route and subsequent loss of r in this particular dialectal form need verification before adding it to either existing flea proposal. Source: schmidt[p. 239, Table 2 item 139];CDIAL[9029, 14711]. |
| Difficult | **tū boʰ, tū bo, bo** ‘you go!’ | 27 | The Backstrom responses are imperative bo, often preceded by tū you, with some recorded final breathiness. Degener explicitly gives bo as the imperative of buj go, whose paradigm also includes suppletive ge/gā forms, while a separate b become verb has the same imperative. Thus the gloss selects the go paradigm, but a direct single-root assignment or use of the become verb merely by surface identity would be misleading; analyze the subject and imperative separately after resolving the go-paradigm parent. Source: backstrom1992;degener-shina2008[p. 252, glossary entry 19];degener-shina2008[p. 246, glossary entry 14]. |
| Difficult | **tūs reʰ, tūs r̃āʰ, tūsa rāʰ, tūs rāʰ, tūse rāʰ, tūse ras, tūs rās, tūse rās, tūsū ras, tūs ras** ‘you speak!’ | 26 | The survey has pronoun + re/rā and ras/rās commands. Degener’s say entry gives r-, ra-, ray- stems and cites 10620, which the compiled graph follows, but Turner’s 10620 explicitly prefers rasati (10652) for Shina; that entry gives Gilgit rayoiki/rām and Kohistani razonũ/Gurezi ražyonũ. Review the compiled base ancestry and the s-bearing command morphology before extending component links; the issue is a genuine primary-source cross-reference, not just spelling similarity. Source: backstrom1992;degener-shina2008[p. 296, glossary entry 8];CDIAL[10620, 10652]. |
| Difficult | **hūro, hūre** ‘different’ | 4 | The hūro/hūre other adjective invites comparison with CDIAL 434 apara, which records regional hōr/hōro but Shina ora except/apart from. The initial h and ū require a dialect-specific explanation or donor identification before assigning this family; regional semantic similarity alone does not establish direct inheritance. Source: CDIAL[434]. |
| Difficult | **jūdā, jūdā jūdā** ‘different’ | 7 | The jūdā separate responses resemble the Persian/Urdu loan family, but immediate donor and route have not been verified here. CDIAL 10497 yuta instead gives Shina yulo/yūlo with an l-extension; assigning the d-forms to that Shina branch would erase the key consonantal difference. Repeated jūdā jūdā also requires an ordered repetition analysis after identifying the base. Source: backstrom1992;CDIAL[10497]. |
| Difficult | **calo, cʰelo, cʰele cʰele, cʰele, cele** ‘different’ | 8 | The cele/chele/calo different family and its repetitions need lexical-source confirmation of their ancestry and relation across dialects. CDIAL chagala goat yields superficially similar chela forms but the meaning supplies no defensible connection; neither this nor the known muto other family should be imposed by resemblance. Source: backstrom1992;CDIAL[4963]. |
| Difficult | **lō̌śī, lāeś, lohāś, lā̌śī, losī, lē̌śī, loāśī, laśī, lahoś, lāśī, loāś, lahāśī, lāeśī, lūhāśī, lūāśī** ‘broom’ | 27 | The survey layshi/loshi/loashi family is supported by Degener leéši long twig broom and Zoller loéši small broom used by the miller, explicitly compared with Shina layēši. Zoller also connects G lhōš with other Indus Kohistani broom forms, but these comparisons supply no deeper ancestry or borrowing direction. Preserve the lVsh family as a research group; the related function does not justify assigning it to an unrelated generic broom root. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 282, glossary entry 6];zoller2005[pp. 369, 371–372];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **śā̌ko, śāko, šā̀ko** ‘arm’ | 27 | Degener šáako arm explicitly compares Burushaski -śák; survey śāko/śā̌ko forms agree. The installed Burushaski candidate f_y4xtg7wrk2nye śåk has English poor, although the preserved German is Arm arm: a translation error, not a semantic obstacle in the source. The main Berger OCR also merges the arm head into an adjacent entry and loses its initial ś. Verify and repair the donor identity and read the complete borrowing discussion before choosing direction or extending links across dialects; no lexical ingestion or compiled-node change is made in this review. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 301, glossary entry 14];berger-auto[arm entry, OCR around printed pp. 388–390];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **desī bālūgun, ṣiṇō balūgūṇ, ṣinō bālūgun, ŋarpāɣān, ṣīno bālugun, moro bālugun, ṣīno bālūgun, moro bālūgun, jono bālogūn, ṣīne bālūgun, ṣinō bolūgun, ṣīnā̌ bālogan, ṣinā bālūgun** ‘eggplant’ | 16 | Most responses modify the independently attested tomato word bālūgun. ṣino may be the relational Shin adjective, whose form is documented under CDIAL 12719, but its culinary meaning here requires confirmation; desi, moro and jono modifiers are not interchangeable without lexical evidence. Degener balúgun tomato compares Burushaski balógan and 11503.3, whereas Turner’s 11503.3 itself cites Bengali bāṛiṅgan and the separate Shina bhatiṅgar is explicitly mediated through Iranian. Therefore the existing direct 11503 links do not settle the local donor route. Existing kīṇo bālūgun whole-phrase links should also be reviewed for ordered components. Hold these responses rather than extending an undifferentiated whole-phrase reflex. Source: backstrom1992;degener-shina2008[p. 248, glossary entry 11];CDIAL[11503, 12719]. |
| Difficult | **ḍāko, ḍakī, ḍākī** ‘hammer’ | 5 | The survey ḍākī/ḍāko/ḍakī hammer forms resemble Degener ḍak hammer blow and Turner addendum 14641, which connects Shina ḍak thoiki with 6701 dhakk. Zoller p. 220 compares Burushaski ḍak, while Berger has a nearby duáko hammer derivative. The nominal endings and long vowel may be local derivation or contact forms; the present evidence does not decide the immediate source. Do not automatically convert an expressive hammering stem into an inherited instrument noun. Source: degener-shina2008;zoller2005[p. 220];berger-auto[duáko hammer];CDIAL[6701, 14641]. |
| Difficult | **ʦātak, ʦaṭak, cātak, ʦatak, ʦaṭko, ʦātko, ʦāṭak, ʦāṭko** ‘hammer’ | 11 | Zoller pp. 159–160 records Indus Kohistani ċaṭakh small hammer and explicitly derives diminutive ċaṭkū/ċaṭkō from it; he compares Pashto ċaṭakai and Kalasha čoṭa, connecting the family with cuṭṭ 4857. The Shina survey ṭ/t and affricate variants are promising matches, including possible diminutives, but the donor route and the exact vowel/suffix development remain unresolved. Turner 4857.2 has no Shina hammer citation, while its Shina čoṭ blow belongs to subsection 5; those branches must not be conflated. Source: zoller2005[pp. 159–160];CDIAL[4857];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **dāl, talcīrī** ‘ash’ | 20 | Degener dáal ash supports the widespread survey dāl identity but gives only Atlas 26, without a historical derivation. The single talcīrī response is structurally different and requires separate segmentation. Turner’s other Shina ash words and Zoller’s Kohistani ash families do not establish the ancestry of dāl; preserve these unresolved rather than substituting a general ash root. Source: degener-shina2008[dáal, Atlas 26];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **cal, cʷel, cvelūko, calbūj, cvel, cālbūc, calbūjī̌, cāltī, cʷal** ‘morning’ | 15 | Degener čalbuǰii early morning and čalbuǰčal early morning support the survey calbuji/calbuǰ forms as a family. The shorter cal/cvel/cwal forms may belong with it, but the source excerpt gives no etymology or segmentation; in particular buj should not be identified with the go verb solely from spelling. Keep the shorter and longer forms distinguishable, pending Atlas or grammar evidence for the formation. Source: degener-shina2008[čalbuǰii, čalbuǰčal];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **dāzo, dāyō̌, dāzō̌, źāo, dāzō, dāeźā, dazo, dāzūko, dāyō** ‘noon’ | 28 | The widespread dāzo/dāyo/dāezā noon set is not explained by the checked Degener and Turner entries. Differences in z/y/ź and the extended dāzūko form must be accounted for before inheritance or local derivation can be proposed. The unrelated-looking ɣārma responses are excluded from this family; the generic meaning noon does not justify attaching these forms to a day or heat root. Source: backstrom1992;Degener time-of-day entries checked;CDIAL comparison checked. |
| Difficult | **tū eʰ, vāʰ, tū vāʰ, e, tū vās, tū vas, eʰ, vas** ‘you come!’ | 37 | The survey has vā/vas/vās and e commands, with or without tū. Degener wa- come has wa-/wai- stems and cites both 884 and 2207. Turner explicitly cross-references avāpayati and upayāti as alternatives for the same Shina verb, including vatu came; neither reference supersedes the other. The e command set also requires a paradigm-specific account rather than being forced under wa-. Review the competing origins and the s-bearing commands before assigning ancestry or component links; do not use unrelated wa heal or an extracted compound fragment as the base. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 312, glossary entry 11];CDIAL[884, 2207];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **áẓo** ‘rain, moisture; cloud’ | 1 | Degener explicitly offers 549 abhra or 1340 ardra for this masculine rain/moisture/cloud entry. Turner records near-identical Shina aẓu under each, but with cloud/rain versus wet meanings; the source’s combined nominal senses do not justify pretending that one alternative has been excluded. Preserve the competing analyses, and keep this record separate from narrowly rain- or wet-glossed attestations. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 246, glossary entry 13];CDIAL[549, 1340]. |
| Difficult | **kʰā́ṣ tʰ** ‘stroke’ | 1 | Degener’s khaṣ th- stroke is a light-verb expression compared with Indus Kohistani khaṣ cleanliness and related to 2972 kaṣati scratch/rub. Turner has no Shina citation here and explicitly questions the expressive aspiration in the comparable Khowar khoṣik groom a horse. The nominal/adverbial first component and the source of its aspiration require review; do not attach the complete expression directly to the Sanskrit verb or substitute an unrelated khaṣ homonym. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 280, glossary entry 1];CDIAL[2972]. |
| Difficult | **abā̀k** ‘wordless, mute’ | 1 | Degener abāk wordless/mute resembles Turner 882a avākka speechless, which only cites Bengali abāk and calls its direct derivation doubtful. No independent Shina speech noun bāk has been verified; the Dras estate homonym is unrelated. Both a historical connection and a loan route require evidence, so a local a- + bāk segmentation is not yet justified. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 243, glossary entry 3];CDIAL[882a]. |
| Difficult | **abṓm** ‘the wrong way around, wrong’ | 1 | Degener abōm wrong-way-around/wrong has a Burushaski parallel: Berger aboóm unlucky, ill-omened, opposed to suboóm, explicitly compares Shina aboóm. The semantic correspondence and the Burushaski opposite are useful leads, but neither establishes donor direction or a Shina negative base bom. Keep the contact family unresolved. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 243, glossary entry 6];berger-auto[printed p. 11, aboóm]. |
| Difficult | **carkaṭij** ‘be jammed, pinched’ | 1 | Degener explicitly labels carkaṭij be jammed/pinched as a passive of carkaṭ crush, and compares Burushaski čarkaṭ man be wounded. The Shina base is only supplied in explanatory prose and has no verified standalone node here; the neighboring carkaṭi blows is a different nominal formation. Preserve the supported passive analysis, pending a source-grounded base identity and its contact history. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 254, glossary entry 20]. |
| Difficult | **borono, barono, barondo, bor̃ono** ‘ring’ | 10 | Degener barono ring compares Burushaski burondo and gives hatei barono finger-ring. The matching survey barono/barondo/borono forms preserve n/nd and rhotic differences. The comparison supports a contact family, but immediate donor identity and direction still require verification; do not merge this family with the finger-derived aŋg-/hag- names. Source: degener-shina2008[baróno];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **hagīlo, āŋsarī, āŋelo, hāksārī, hāgūlelo, haguzelo, āŋẽlo, hāɣuelo, hūɣūṛelū, hagsarī, hāksarī, āŋvelo, hāgūvelo, āŋgūele, hagūzarī, āŋʷelo, hāguelo, āksarī** ‘ring’ | 23 | The aŋg-/hag- ring set invites comparison with aṅgulīya 136 and aṅguṣṭhiya 138, but its -l/-r and -sari extensions cannot all be treated as one inherited shape. Zoller pp. 63, 75 explicitly derives Indus Kohistani aṅguili ring from 136, while his aṅgṭhui belongs to 138; the Shina forms with h, velar weakening or -sari need their own correspondence or donor analysis. Preserve the two historical candidates and investigate the internal subgroups before assigning a common parent. Source: backstrom1992;zoller2005[pp. 63–75, 406];CDIAL[136, 138]. |
| Difficult | **bé̃ye** ‘both’ | 1 | Degener bẽye both lacks an etymological attribution. Turner 6648 gives Shina du two and Sindhi bei both, but a b-initial parallel in another language is not sufficient to explain this Shina form, including its nasalization and ending. Zoller’s Kohistani duyaṽ/bhaždo/sab-du both expressions also do not establish the missing bridge; keep this form unassigned. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 251, glossary entry 5];CDIAL[6648];zoller2005[pp. 250, 319–324, 394]. |
| Difficult | **ʦ̣ʰīlo, ʦ̣ʰīle, ʦ̣ʰile, ʦ̣īle, ʦ̣ʰilīkʰoī, ṣīle, cīlo, ʦ̣īlo** ‘cloth; garment, clothing’ | 16 | Degener c̣hīlo garment/clothing refers to Buddruss 1996 p. 42 without supplying a derivation. The survey’s c̣hīlo/c̣īle/ṣīle and cīlo forms provide a dialectal family, with initial articulation and adjective/noun endings retained, but a cīra rag connection has not been verified. In particular Turner 4844 concerns a splitting verb and explicitly marks its Shina affricate form as a loan; it is not direct evidence for these cloth nouns. The extended c̣hilīkhoi response needs component checking as well. Await the cited discussion before assigning a historical head. Source: degener-shina2008[p. 258, glossary entry 9];Buddruss 1996 p. 42 (follow-up reference, full discussion not available);CDIAL[4844];backstrom1992. |
| Difficult | **pʰaṭālo, faṭālo, paṭālo, pʰaṛālo, pʰaṭʰalī, patālo, pʰāṭalī, patʰālo, paṭalī, paṭʰālo, paṭālī, faṛālo, patālī** ‘leg’ | 25 | Degener phaṭāli thigh compares Kohistani phäṭël, and Zoller p. 288 phäṭël thigh explicitly cites Shina phaṭālu thigh. The survey’s paṭali/phaṭali/phaṛalo etc. use the broader leg gloss; preserve that elicitation and the aspiration, retroflexion and ending variation. Neither checked source supplies a deeper origin. A resemblance to pāda/tala or paṭala does not explain the full shape and should not be promoted to an inherited compound without further evidence. Source: backstrom1992;degener-shina2008[phaṭaáli];zoller2005[p. 288]. |
